[Director 指令] [Patch 补充] P2 (340M) 整合时的两个注意点（Reviewer 刚确认）：

1. **340M α 跨 ratio 变化 = 0.047，超出正文 "variation < 0.03" 的 claim**。该 claim 在 §4.2 仅针对 1.3B。整合 340M 时：在 appendix 小节中注明 340M α range 较大（0.047 vs 1.3B 的 0.022），可能反映 smaller models 对 attention ratio 更敏感。正文 §4.2 的 "< 0.03" claim 不需要改（scope 已限定 1.3B）。

2. **attention_ratios 数值**：分析代码算出 h3-1 = 0.333（8/24 layers），论文定义 h3-1 = 0.25（1/(3+1) interleaving）。不影响 ρ（Spearman 只看 rank），但 appendix 表格中 ratio 列要用论文定义（0.25），不要直接用代码输出的 0.333。

540	\section{340M LAMBADA Per-Example Analysis}\label{app:scale_per_example}
541	
542	At 340M, LAMBADA per-example multiplicity closely matches the 1.3B pattern: $\alpha = 0.313$ (vs.\ 0.307 at 1.3B), with mean pairwise Jaccard $\bar{J} \approx 0.089$ (vs.\ 0.095). Per-example directionality is preserved: HGRN2 shows net positive effects at h3-1 ($+238$ examples), while GDN ($-256$) and RetNet ($-106$) show net negative effects, mirroring the 1.3B profile (HGRN2 $+153$, GDN $-153$, RetNet $-69$).
543	
544	However, aggregate convergence is absent at 340M: $\rho = -0.50$ ($p = .45$), with non-monotonic $\sigma$ across attention ratios ($0.025 \to 0.012 \to 0.018 \to 0.011 \to 0.016$). This contrasts sharply with 1.3B ($\rho = -1.0$, $p = .017$, monotonic $\sigma$ decrease). The decoupling between per-example divergence and aggregate convergence thus manifests differently across scales: at 340M, per-example multiplicity is present but aggregate convergence is not, suggesting that convergence requires sufficient model capacity while multiplicity does not.
545

547	\centering
548	\small
549	\caption{340M vs.\ 1.3B LAMBADA comparison.}
550	\label{tab:scale_comparison}

554	\midrule
555	$\alpha$ (pure) & .313 & .307 \\
556	$\bar{J}$ (h3-1) & .089 & .095 \\
557	$\rho_\sigma$ & $-0.50$ & $-1.0$ \\
558	$p$ & .45 & .017 \\
559	GDN net & $-256$ & $-153$ \\
560	HGRN2 net & $+238$ & $+153$ \\

563	\end{table}

Per-example directionality is preserved: HGRN2 shows net positive effects at h3-1 ($+238$ examples), while GDN ($-256$) and RetNet ($-106$) show net negative effects, mirroring the 1.3B profile (HGRN2 $+153$, GDN $-153$, RetNet $-69$).\n\nHowever, aggregate convergence is absent at 340M: $\\rho = -0.50$ ($p = .45$), with non-monotonic $\\sigma$ across attention ratios ($0.025 \\to 0.012 \\to 0.018 \\to 0.011 \\to 0.016$). This contrasts sharply with 1.3B ($\\rho = -1.0$, $p = .017$, monotonic $\\sigma$ decrease). The decoupling between per-example divergence and aggregate convergence thus manifests differently across scales: at 340M, per-example multiplicity is present but aggregate convergence is not, suggesting that convergence requires sufficient model capacity while multiplicity does not.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{340M vs.\\ 1.3B LAMBADA comparison.}\n\\label{tab:scale_comparison}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n & 340M & 1.3B \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\alpha$ (pure) & .313 & .307 \\\\\n$\\bar{J}$ (h3-1) & .089 & .095 \\\\\n$\\rho_\\sigma$ & $-0.50$ & $-1.0$ \\\\\n$p$ & .45 & .017 \\\\\nGDN net & $-256$ & $-153$ \\\\\nHGRN2 net & $+238$ & $+153$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Subsample Sensitivity of \\texorpdfstring{$\\alpha$}{α}}\\label{app:subsample_sensitivity}\n\nTo assess the reliability of $\\alpha$ estimation at different sample sizes, we repeatedly subsample each benchmark (1{,}000 bootstrap replicates, seed 42) and compute the 95\\% CI width of $\\alpha$.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]",

      "totalLines": 588
    }

345	Per-example effect sets exhibit a three-tier Jaccard hierarchy on LAMBADA (1.3B scale). Under independence (permutation baseline), pairwise Jaccard is $J \approx 0.05$. Cross-architecture comparisons (different architectures at the same attention ratio) yield $J \approx 0.10$, approximately $2\times$ baseline. Within-architecture comparisons (same architecture across different attention ratios) yield $J \approx 0.33$, approximately $7\times$ baseline and $3\times$ the cross-architecture level. Architecture identity therefore has approximately three times the influence of attention dose on per-example effects.
346	
347	Table~\ref{tab:within_arch_summary} reports mean within-architecture Jaccard for each architecture. HGRN2 shows the highest helped overlap and lowest hurt overlap, consistent with its monotonically increasing helped count across ratios (479 $\to$ 548).
348

  "new_string": "However, aggregate convergence is absent at 340M: $\\rho = -0.50$ ($p = .45$), with non-monotonic $\\sigma$ across attention ratios ($0.025 \\to 0.012 \\to 0.018 \\to 0.011 \\to 0.016$). This contrasts sharply with 1.3B ($\\rho = -1.0$, $p = .017$, monotonic $\\sigma$ decrease). ### Tool result

_source line 3373 · 2026-05-21T14:19:03.480Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BaXmBhxZvfQVtJ5sXKFSdr`</summary>

```text
44	Do architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped (correct only in hybrid), hurt (correct only in pure), or unchanged.
45	
46	Each architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Table~\ref{tab:per_example}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which attention produces a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\bar{J} \leq 0.104$ for helped, $\leq 0.096$ for hurt, indicating ${\sim}90$\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\text{null}} \approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), but normalizing by the theoretical upper bound $J_{\max} = \min(|A|,|B|)/\max(|A|,|B|)$, observed overlap reaches only 5--7\% of the achievable range above chance (normalized ES 0.065 helped, 0.062 hurt; per-pair details in Appendix~\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.
47	
48	No surface-level feature (token count, word frequency, syntactic complexity) reliably separates helped from hurt examples. However, pure-model confidence on the target token is strongly predictive within each architecture: hurt examples have lower target-token probability among pure-correct examples ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$), and helped examples have higher probability among pure-wrong examples ($d = 0.73$--$0.77$); see Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}. A Kruskal-Wallis test confirms cross-architecture structure in both hurt ($p = .0005$) and helped ($p = .020$) distributions. The confidence margin thus identifies a per-example predictor of which examples will be affected: attention disrupts marginally correct predictions and rescues near-miss errors, but with architecture-specific thresholds that produce the observed cross-architecture divergence. The rescue pattern generalizes: across all three architectures on LAMBADA, HellaSwag, and ARC-Easy, helped examples have significantly higher pure-model target probability than stayed-wrong examples (9/9 tests, $d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}). The disruption pattern is weaker on ARC-Easy ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$), absent on HellaSwag under \texttt{acc\_norm}, and absent on binary classification (Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}), likely reflecting structural properties of constrained-choice formats that limit the discriminative power of confidence margins. This structured per-example disagreement despite similar aggregate performance constitutes predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} arising from architectural variation rather than random initialization \citep{damour2022underspecification}.
49	
50	\paragraph{Multiplicity spectrum.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent spectrum. Evaluation format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way, $\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (two-choice, $\alpha = 0.14$) produce nearly identical multiplicity despite different formats; among two-choice benchmarks alone, $\alpha$ spans $3\times$ from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.41 (WinoGrande; Appendix~\ref{app:winogrande}) to 0.45 (BoolQ; Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}), covering the full six-benchmark range within a single format. Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm the spectrum ordering: all adjacent-benchmark CIs are non-overlapping except PIQA and HellaSwag, whose CIs overlap substantially ($[.127,.159]$ vs.\ $[.141,.155]$; Appendix, Table~\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.
51	
52	\begin{table}[t]

The confidence margin thus identifies a per-example predictor of which examples will be affected: attention disrupts marginally correct predictions and rescues near-miss errors, but with architecture-specific thresholds that produce the observed cross-architecture divergence. The rescue pattern generalizes: across all three architectures on LAMBADA, HellaSwag, and ARC-Easy, helped examples have significantly higher pure-model target probability than stayed-wrong examples (9/9 tests, $d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}). The disruption pattern is weaker on ARC-Easy ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$), absent on HellaSwag under \\texttt{acc\\_norm}, and absent on binary classification (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}), likely reflecting structural properties of constrained-choice formats that limit the discriminative power of confidence margins. This structured per-example disagreement despite similar aggregate performance constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} arising from architectural variation rather than random initialization \\citep{damour2022underspecification}.\n\n\\paragraph{Multiplicity spectrum.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent spectrum. Evaluation format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way, $\\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (two-choice, $\\alpha = 0.14$) produce nearly identical multiplicity despite different formats; among two-choice benchmarks alone, $\\alpha$ spans $3\\times$ from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.41 (WinoGrande; Appendix~\\ref{app:winogrande}) to 0.45 (BoolQ; Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}), covering the full six-benchmark range within a single format. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm the spectrum ordering: all adjacent-benchmark CIs are non-overlapping except PIQA and HellaSwag, whose CIs overlap substantially ($[.127,.159]$ vs.\\ $[.141,.155]$; Appendix, Table~\\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \\citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering",

      "totalLines": 84
    }

Additionally, we report continuous-valued analyses that are consistent with the binary results: Cohen's $d$ for pure-model confidence margins ranges from 0.73 to 0.92 between helped and hurt examples on LAMBADA (Appendix, ``Confidence Margin Analysis''), showing that the binary decomposition captures a real separation in the continuous confidence space. We have extended this analysis to all four benchmarks:}
109	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
110	\item \textbf{Rescue pattern (C2)}: Near-miss errors rescued by attention replicate across all three main benchmarks with confidence margin data (9/9 tests significant; $d = 0.13$--$0.17$ on HellaSwag under \texttt{acc\_norm}, $d = 0.51$--$0.60$ on ARC-Easy, $d = 0.73$--$0.77$ on LAMBADA). On BoolQ (binary classification), the rescue signal is weaker but directionally consistent (2/3, $d = 0.16$--$0.36$).
111	\item \textbf{Disruption pattern (C1)}: Effect sizes decrease with answer-space dimensionality: open-ended prediction ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$, LAMBADA) $>$ ARC-Easy ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$) $>$ HellaSwag ($d \approx 0$ under \texttt{acc\_norm}) $\approx$ binary ($d \approx 0$, BoolQ). This gradient broadly tracks the coefficient of variation (CV) of the log-likelihood confidence proxy: LAMBADA (CV $= 0.75$) $>$ ARC-Easy ($0.54$) $>$ HellaSwag ($0.49$) $>$ BoolQ ($0.22$). BoolQ's CV is $3.4\times$ lower than LAMBADA's, leaving insufficient relative spread to distinguish borderline-correct from robustly-correct examples. The asymmetry with C2 is expected: a correct answer in multiple-choice settings can be robust despite low absolute confidence if all alternatives score worse, whereas near-miss errors remain identifiable by relatively high target probability regardless of task format.
112	\end{itemize}
113	Critically, the multiplicity findings themselves---ambiguity $\alpha$ and pairwise Jaccard---do not depend on the confidence margin analysis. Multiplicity is present and statistically significant on all four benchmarks including BoolQ ($\alpha = 0.45$, 12/24 Jaccard pairs significant). The confidence margin provides a partial mechanistic account of \emph{why} certain examples are affected, but the \emph{existence} of per-example multiplicity is established independently.
114	
115	The Jaccard similarity measure applied to the binary sets provides an interpretable metric of cross-architecture overlap that would be less transparent with continuous alternatives.
116

The Conclusion calls for extending the analysis ``beyond these three architectures.'' We believe the controlled design is the appropriate choice for a first systematic study, while recognizing that confirmation across additional model families is needed.}

It is discussed in three places: (1) the Method section describes the exact permutation test with 120 permutations; (2) \\S4.1 reports the BCa bootstrap CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$ as a complementary characterization alongside the $p$-value; (3) the Limitations section (``Statistical power'' paragraph) explicitly states the minimum achievable $p$-value ceiling and its consequences. We agree that this limits the strength of the convergence finding, which is why we have removed convergence from the title and weakened all convergence-related claims (see W-OVERCLAIM above). The per-example multiplicity finding, which is the paper's central contribution, does not depend on the convergence $p$-value.}\n\n\\location{\\S3.5, \\S4.1 (paragraph 1), Limitations (``Statistical power'' paragraph).}\n\n\\subsection*{R3-BINARY (R1-W5): Binary correct/incorrect decomposition}\n\n\\reviewer{The helped/hurt decomposition uses binary correctness, which may lose information compared to continuous confidence scores.}\n\n\\response{The binary decomposition is a deliberate methodological choice aligned with the predictive multiplicity framework of Marx et al.\\ (2020), which defines multiplicity over discrete prediction outcomes. Additionally, we report continuous-valued analyses that are consistent with the binary results: Cohen's $d$ for pure-model confidence margins ranges from 0.73 to 0.92 between helped and hurt examples on LAMBADA (Appendix, ``Confidence Margin Analysis''), showing that the binary decomposition captures a real separation in the continuous confidence space. We have extended this analysis to all four benchmarks:}\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]\n\\item \\textbf{Rescue pattern (C2)}: Near-miss errors rescued by attention replicate across all three main benchmarks with confidence margin data (9/9 tests significant; $d = 0.13$--$0.17$ on HellaSwag under \\texttt{acc\\_norm}, $d = 0.51$--$0.60$ on ARC-Easy, $d = 0.73$--$0.77$ on LAMBADA). On BoolQ (binary classification), the rescue signal is weaker but directionally consistent (2/3, $d = 0.16$--$0.36$).\n\\item \\textbf{Disruption pattern (C1)}: Effect sizes decrease with answer-space dimensionality: open-ended prediction ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$, LAMBADA) $>$ ARC-Easy ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$) $>$ HellaSwag ($d \\approx 0$ under \\texttt{acc\\_norm}) $\\approx$ binary ($d \\approx 0$, BoolQ). This gradient broadly tracks the coefficient of variation (CV) of the log-likelihood confidence proxy: LAMBADA (CV $= 0.75$) $>$ ARC-Easy ($0.54$) $>$ HellaSwag ($0.49$) $>$ BoolQ ($0.22$). BoolQ's CV is $3.4\\times$ lower than LAMBADA's, leaving insufficient relative spread to distinguish borderline-correct from robustly-correct examples. The asymmetry with C2 is expected: a correct answer in multiple-choice settings can be robust despite low absolute confidence if all alternatives score worse, whereas near-miss errors remain identifiable by relatively high target probability regardless of task format.\n\\end{itemize}\nCritically, the multiplicity findings themselves---ambiguity $\\alpha$ and pairwise Jaccard---do not depend on the confidence margin analysis. Multiplicity is present and statistically significant on all four benchmarks including BoolQ ($\\alpha = 0.45$, 12/24 Jaccard pairs significant). The confidence margin provides a partial mechanistic account of \\emph{why} certain examples are affected, but the \\emph{existence} of per-example multiplicity is established independently.\n\nThe Jaccard similarity measure applied to the binary sets provides an interpretable metric of cross-architecture overlap that would be less transparent with continuous alternatives.\n\n\\location{\\S4.3 (confidence margin paragraph), Appendix (Confidence Margin Analysis, Cross-Benchmark Extension, BoolQ Supplementary Analysis).}\n\n\\subsection*{W-ZOO (R1-W6): Reliance on a single model zoo}\n\n\\reviewer{All results depend on one model zoo; the controlled design limits external validity.}\n\n\\response{We acknowledge this trade-off explicitly. The controlled design of the zoo---identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters across all configurations---is what allows us to isolate architectural effects from training confounds. This is stated in \\S3.1 and in the Limitations section (``Architecture coverage'' paragraph), which now also notes: ``Each configuration in the model zoo corresponds to a single training run; we cannot disentangle architectural effects from seed-specific effects.'' The Conclusion calls for extending the analysis ``beyond these three architectures.'' We believe the controlled design is the appropriate choice for a first systematic study, while recognizing that confirmation across additional model families is needed.}\n\n\\location{\\S3.1, Limitations (``Architecture coverage''), \\S5.}\n\n\\subsection*{Q1: p-value rounding discrepancy (0.100 vs.\\ 0.102)}\n\n\\reviewer{Why does the Bonferroni-adjusted p-value appear as both 0.100 and 0.102 in different places?}\n\n\\response{We have added a footnote to Table~1 explaining the discrepancy: ``Bonferroni-adjusted $p = 6 \\times 2/120 = 0.100$ (exact); 0.102 arises from rounding raw $p$ to .017 before multiplying.'' The value 0.100 is the exact result; 0.102 is a rounding artifact. We now use 0.100 consistently throughout.}\n\n\\location{Table~1 footnote.}\n\n\\subsection*{Q5: Single checkpoint per configuration}\n\n\\reviewer{Each model zoo configuration is a single training run. Can you disentangle architecture from seed?}\n\n\\response{We cannot, and we now state this explicitly in the Limitations section: ``Each configuration in the model zoo corresponds to a single training run; we cannot disentangle architectural effects from seed-specific effects.'' This is a limitation of the zoo design. Disentangling the two would require multiple seeds per architecture-attention configuration, which is beyond the scope of the available zoo. We note, however, that the zoo's controlled design (shared data, hyperparameters) reduces other confounds, making architecture the most salient axis of variation.}\n\n\\location{Limitations (``Architecture coverage'' paragraph).}\n\n\\subsection*{W-REFS (R2-W2): Additional related work citations}\n\n\\reviewer{Several relevant references are missing from the related work discussion.}\n\n\\response{We have substantially expanded the citation coverage. In addition to Sellam et al.\\ (ICLR 2022; MultiBERTs) and Fehlauer et al.\\ (EMNLP 2025) added in Round~2, we now cite five additional references:}\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]\n\\item \\textbf{Predictive Multiplicity paragraph}: Du et al.\\ (2025; reconciliation under multiplicity), Haghighat et al.\\ (2026; Rashomon-set resolution), and Gorbett \\& Jana (2026; cross-model disagreement as label-free correctness signal).\n\\item \\textbf{Hybrid Architectures paragraph}: Ali et al.\\ (2025; implicit attention in Mamba) and Lahoti et al.\\ (2026; Mamba-3).\n\\item \\textbf{Appendix~D}: Ali et al.\\ (2025) cited as methodological background for attention weight analysis.\n\\item \\textbf{\\S4.3}: Gorbett \\& Jana (2026) cited for the practical relevance of per-example disagreement.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\location{\\S2 (``Predictive Multiplicity'' and ``Hybrid Recurrent Architectures'' paragraphs), \\S4.3, Appendix~D, \\texttt{references.bib}.}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

```text
48	No surface-level feature (token count, word frequency, syntactic complexity) reliably separates helped from hurt examples. However, pure-model confidence on the target token is strongly predictive within each architecture: hurt examples have lower target-token probability among pure-correct examples ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$), and helped examples have higher probability among pure-wrong examples ($d = 0.73$--$0.77$); see Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}. A Kruskal-Wallis test confirms cross-architecture structure in both hurt ($p = .0005$) and helped ($p = .020$) distributions. The confidence margin thus identifies a per-example predictor of which examples will be affected: attention disrupts marginally correct predictions and rescues near-miss errors, but with architecture-specific thresholds that produce the observed cross-architecture divergence. The rescue pattern generalizes: across all three architectures on LAMBADA, HellaSwag, and ARC-Easy, helped examples have significantly higher pure-model target probability than stayed-wrong examples (9/9 tests, $d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}). The disruption pattern is weaker on ARC-Easy ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$), absent on HellaSwag under \texttt{acc\_norm}, and absent on binary classification (Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}), likely reflecting structural properties of constrained-choice formats that limit the discriminative power of confidence margins. This structured per-example disagreement despite similar aggregate performance constitutes predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} arising from architectural variation rather than random initialization \citep{damour2022underspecification}.
49	
50	\paragraph{Multiplicity spectrum.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent spectrum. Evaluation format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way, $\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (two-choice, $\alpha = 0.14$) produce nearly identical multiplicity despite different formats; among two-choice benchmarks alone, $\alpha$ spans $3\times$ from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.41 (WinoGrande; Appendix~\ref{app:winogrande}) to 0.45 (BoolQ; Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}), covering the full six-benchmark range within a single format. Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm the spectrum ordering: all adjacent-benchmark CIs are non-overlapping except PIQA and HellaSwag, whose CIs overlap substantially ($[.127,.159]$ vs.\ $[.141,.155]$; Appendix, Table~\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.
51	
52	\begin{table}[t]

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Rm7y4heJmJvKZZwaLKSK3q`</summary>

```text
74	
75	Cross-architecture $\sigma$ decreases gradually across the five attention ratios: $0.024 \to 0.016 \to 0.014 \to 0.006 \to 0.005$. In contrast, pairwise Jaccard similarity between architectures' per-example effects saturates at the first attention level (4\%): approximately 0.095 for helped, with no further increase at higher ratios. Net asymmetry between architectures' per-example profiles increases from 0.013 at 4\% attention to 0.040 at 25\%.
76	
77	This separation of timescales constitutes a decoupling: attention reduces the aggregate performance gap but does not homogenize per-example behavior. Bootstrap confidence intervals (10{,}000 resamples) confirm this statistically: the 95\% CI for $\sigma$ does not overlap between pure $[0.019, 0.030]$ and h3-1 $[0.002, 0.011]$, while the CIs for mean Jaccard overlap completely across all non-zero attention ratios (helped: $[0.08, 0.11]$; hurt: $[0.07, 0.11]$). Within-architecture Jaccard averages ${\sim}0.33$ (Appendix~\ref{app:within_arch_stability}), roughly $3\times$ the cross-architecture value (${\sim}0.10$). Attributing this gap entirely to seed effects would require seed variation to exceed the effect of replacing 25\% of model layers with attention---implausible though not directly testable with the available data.
78	
79	Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark spectrum (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}) confirms independence: LAMBADA ($\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing convergence despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\alpha = 0.15$) show no convergence with the lowest multiplicity. At 340M, LAMBADA per-example multiplicity is comparable ($\alpha = 0.31$, $\bar{J} \approx 0.09$) but aggregate convergence is absent ($\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$), further confirming that per-example divergence is stable across scales while aggregate convergence requires sufficient capacity (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_per_example}).
80	
81	\subsection{Robustness}\label{ssec:robustness}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_012e7V7dCYcD19u2wMSbUsJd`</summary>

```text
16	\end{figure}
17	
18	Our analysis yields three findings. First, attention-driven convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$; cross-architecture $\sigma$ drops 79\%). LAMBADA \citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\rho| \leq 0.7$ and $p \geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is scale-dependent: monotonic at 1.3B but non-monotonic at 340M (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_convergence}). Second, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent spectrum. The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\% attention (${\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).
19	
20	Our contributions are as follows:

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01LVjFVFEeT7ALPRMwfWPJCM`</summary>

```text
5	\subsection{Task-Specific Convergence}\label{ssec:task_convergence}
6	
7	Table~\ref{tab:convergence} reports cross-architecture $\sigma$ at each attention ratio for all benchmarks at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA is the only benchmark exhibiting monotonic convergence: $\rho = -1.0$ ($p = .017$; BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$; $\sigma$ drops 79\%; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$). This is consistent with attention compensating for differences in how recurrent architectures retrieve from context \citep{arora2024zoology}---LAMBADA requires predicting a final word from long-range dependencies, and attention provides direct token-level access that narrows architectural differences in recall capacity \citep{arora2024based}. No other benchmark shows significant convergence (all $|\rho| \leq 0.7$, $p \geq .233$; Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}). Full convergence discussion appears in Appendix~\ref{app:convergence_detail}. Scale analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_convergence}) shows this convergence is monotonic at 1.3B but non-monotonic at 340M.
8	
```

394	
395	\section{Target-Token Confidence Margin}
396	\label{app:confidence_margin}
397	
398	We analyze whether pure-model confidence on the target token predicts whether attention will help or hurt on a given example.
399	We define confidence as $|\log p_{\text{pure}}(y^*)|$, where $y^*$ is the target token: lower values indicate higher probability (near-correct).
400	To avoid the definitional confound of comparing pure-correct (hurt) against pure-wrong (helped) samples, we perform within-category controls.
401	
402	Table~\ref{tab:cm_c1} compares hurt versus stayed-right samples (both pure-correct): hurt samples have significantly lower target-token probability (higher $|\log p|$), confirming that weakly-correct predictions are vulnerable to attention disruption.
403	Table~\ref{tab:cm_c2} compares helped versus stayed-wrong samples (both pure-wrong): helped samples have higher target-token probability (lower $|\log p|$), confirming that near-miss errors are rescuable.
404	This pattern is consistent across all three architectures ($|d| = 0.73$--$0.92$, all $p < 10^{-54}$) but does not alter the cross-architecture Jaccard findings---within-architecture predictability coexists with cross-architecture divergence.
405	
406	\begin{table}[h]
407	\centering
408	\caption{Control 1: Pure-correct samples---hurt vs.\ stayed-right. Higher mean $|\log p|$ indicates lower target-token probability (less confident correct).}
409	\label{tab:cm_c1}
410	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
411	\begin{tabular}{lrrrrr}
412	\toprule
413	Architecture & $n_{\text{hurt}}$ & $n_{\text{stay}}$ & Hurt mean & Stay mean & Cohen's $d$ \\
414	\midrule
415	GDN (h12-1) & 519 & 2281 & 1.049 & 0.576 & 0.87 \\
416	RetNet (h3-1) & 497 & 2169 & 1.293 & 0.785 & 0.83 \\
417	HGRN2 (h3-1) & 392 & 2102 & 1.481 & 0.899 & 0.92 \\
418	\bottomrule
419	\end{tabular}
420	}
421	\end{table}
422

          " Do architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped (correct only in hybrid), hurt (correct only in pure), or unchanged.",
          " ",
          "-Each architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Table~\\ref{tab:per_example}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which attention produces a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\\bar{J} \\leq 0.104$ for helped, $\\leq 0.096$ for hurt, indicating ${\\sim}90$\\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\\text{null}} \\approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), but normalizing by the theoretical upper bound $J_{\\max} = \\min(|A|,|B|)/\\max(|A|,|B|)$, observed overlap reaches only 5--7\\% of the achievable range above chance (normalized ES 0.065 helped, 0.062 hurt; per-pair details in Appendix~\\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.",
          "+Each architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Table~\\ref{tab:per_example}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which attention produces a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\\bar{J} \\leq 0.104$ for helped, $\\leq 0.096$ for hurt, indicating ${\\sim}90$\\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\\text{null}} \\approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), but normalizing by the theoretical upper bound $J_{\\max} = \\min(|A|,|B|)/\\max(|A|,|B|)$, observed overlap reaches only 5--7\\% of the achievable range above chance (normalized ES 0.065 helped, 0.047 hurt; per-pair details in Appendix~\\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.",
          " ",
          " No surface-level feature (token count, word frequency, syntactic complexity) reliably separates helped from hurt examples. However, pure-model confidence on the target token is strongly predictive within each architecture: hurt examples have lower target-token probability among pure-correct examples ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$), and helped examples have higher probability among pure-wrong examples ($d = 0.73$--$0.77$); see Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}. A Kruskal-Wallis test confirms cross-architecture structure in both hurt ($p = .0005$) and helped ($p = .020$) distributions. The confidence margin thus identifies a per-example predictor of which examples will be affected: attention disrupts marginally correct predictions and rescues near-miss errors, but with architecture-specific thresholds that produce the observed cross-architecture divergence. The rescue pattern generalizes: across all three architectures on LAMBADA, HellaSwag, and ARC-Easy, helped examples have significantly higher pure-model target probability than stayed-wrong examples (9/9 tests, $d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}). The disruption pattern is weaker on ARC-Easy ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$), absent on HellaSwag under \\texttt{acc\\_norm}, and absent on binary classification (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}), likely reflecting structural properties of constrained-choice formats that limit the discriminative power of confidence margins. This structured per-example disagreement despite similar aggregate performance constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} arising from architectural variation rather than random initialization \\citep{damour2022underspecification}.",

          " ",
          " No surface-level feature (token count, word frequency, syntactic complexity) reliably separates helped from hurt examples. However, pure-model confidence on the target token is strongly predictive within each architecture: hurt examples have lower target-token probability among pure-correct examples ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$), and helped examples have higher probability among pure-wrong examples ($d = 0.73$--$0.77$); see Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}. A Kruskal-Wallis test confirms cross-architecture structure in both hurt ($p = .0005$) and helped ($p = .020$) distributions. The confidence margin thus identifies a per-example predictor of which examples will be affected: attention disrupts marginally correct predictions and rescues near-miss errors, but with architecture-specific thresholds that produce the observed cross-architecture divergence. The rescue pattern generalizes: across all three architectures on LAMBADA, HellaSwag, and ARC-Easy, helped examples have significantly higher pure-model target probability than stayed-wrong examples (9/9 tests, $d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}). The disruption pattern is weaker on ARC-Easy ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$), absent on HellaSwag under \\texttt{acc\\_norm}, and absent on binary classification (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}), likely reflecting structural properties of constrained-choice formats that limit the discriminative power of confidence margins. This structured per-example disagreement despite similar aggregate performance constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} arising from architectural variation rather than random initialization \\citep{damour2022underspecification}.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Multiplicity spectrum.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent spectrum. Evaluation format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way, $\\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (two-choice, $\\alpha = 0.14$) produce nearly identical multiplicity despite different formats; among two-choice benchmarks alone, $\\alpha$ spans $3\\times$ from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.41 (WinoGrande; Appendix~\\ref{app:winogrande}) to 0.45 (BoolQ; Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}), covering the full six-benchmark range within a single format. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm the spectrum ordering: all adjacent-benchmark CIs are non-overlapping except PIQA and HellaSwag, whose CIs overlap substantially ($[.127,.159]$ vs.\\ $[.141,.155]$; Appendix, Table~\\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \\citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.",
          "+\\paragraph{Multiplicity spectrum.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent spectrum. Evaluation format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way, $\\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (two-choice, $\\alpha = 0.14$) produce nearly identical multiplicity despite different formats; among two-choice benchmarks alone, $\\alpha$ spans $3\\times$ from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.41 (WinoGrande; Appendix~\\ref{app:winogrande}) to 0.45 (BoolQ; Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}), covering the full six-benchmark range within a single format. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm the spectrum ordering: all adjacent-benchmark CIs are non-overlapping except PIQA and HellaSwag, whose CIs overlap substantially ($[.127,\\,.159]$ vs.\\ $[.141,\\,.155]$; Appendix, Table~\\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \\citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.",

<details><summary>Recorded metadata</summary>

```json
{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "44e879da-6799-424e-a83e-b883c1c24b75",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "97bf1d0c-cac2-4347-b03e-ddb82c784535",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "b958070e-14d8-4e70-80ff-002479c67b6a",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "$[-1.0, -0.7]$",
    "newString": "$[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nPractitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores, yet architectures that score identically in aggregate may behave differently on individual inputs---with hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction. When attention layers are added to three different recurrent architectures, their LAMBADA performance becomes near-identical: cross-architecture standard deviation drops from 0.024 to 0.005, a 79\\% reduction (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero). Yet this aggregate convergence hides an asymmetry at the per-example level. The three architectures reach similar overall accuracy by helping and hurting different examples with low overlap (pairwise Jaccard at most 0.11, approximately twice the independence baseline). Two models can agree on a benchmark score while disagreeing on which inputs they get right.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \\citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \\citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks---LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$), PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$), and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$)---we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields three findings. First, attention-driven convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is scale-dependent: monotonic at 1.3B but non-monotonic at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). Second, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent spectrum. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We demonstrate that per-example predictive multiplicity arising from architectural variation---rather than random initialization---spans a task-dependent spectrum ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks spanning three task categories (retrieval, reasoning, mixed) in three recurrent backbones, with magnitude independent of aggregate convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}).\n    \\item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence, and this pattern is scale-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}; \\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

[Director 指令] [Patch] Hostile review 模拟发现的两个写作优化。这次不用验证引用。

**P-HR-1: Introduction 以 multiplicity 而非 convergence 开场**
当前 Introduction 第一段以 convergence 开场（L3: "cross-architecture standard deviation drops from 0.024 to 0.005"），但 convergence 是论文最弱的 claim（Bonferroni p=.100）。如果 multiplicity 是 central contribution，第一段应以 multiplicity 作为 hook。

建议方向（不必逐字照搬）：
"When attention is added to three recurrent architectures, they help and hurt almost entirely different examples (Jaccard ≤ 0.10), even when aggregate accuracy converges." — 先给 multiplicity 的惊人事实，再引出 convergence 作为对比背景。

保留 convergence 的提及但降低其 prominence。不要删除 79% σ reduction 数据，而是将其从"开场 hook"降级为"背景对比"。

**P-HR-2: Contribution list 明确区分 prior work**
当前 contribution list 可能没有明确区分 seed-driven disagreement（McCoy et al. 2020, D'Amour et al. 2022）和 architecture-driven multiplicity。

在 contribution list 中加入或修改一条，明确区分：
"Unlike prior studies that examine seed-driven disagreement within a single architecture, we show that architectural variation—under matched training conditions—produces structured per-example multiplicity with task-dependent magnitude, a phenomenon not addressable by seed averaging."

**约束**：主文 ≤ 8 页。这两个改动是替换/重写，不加篇幅。

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0125xVhPvSJUKbP55FJpNZ9L`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores, yet architectures that score identically in aggregate may behave differently on individual inputs---with hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction. When attention layers are added to three different recurrent architectures, their LAMBADA performance becomes near-identical: cross-architecture standard deviation drops from 0.024 to 0.005, a 79\% reduction (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the BCa 95\% CI excludes zero). Yet this aggregate convergence hides an asymmetry at the per-example level. The three architectures reach similar overall accuracy by helping and hurting different examples with low overlap (pairwise Jaccard at most 0.11, approximately twice the independence baseline). Two models can agree on a benchmark score while disagreeing on which inputs they get right.
4	
5	This tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.
6	
7	Most evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.
8	
9	We present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\% to 25\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks---LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$), PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$), and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$)---we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.
10	
11	\begin{figure}[t]
12	\centering
13	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}
14	\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\%) and remains flat.}
15	\label{fig:decoupling}
16	\end{figure}
17	
18	Our analysis yields three findings. First, attention-driven convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0,\,-0.7]$; cross-architecture $\sigma$ drops 79\%). LAMBADA \citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\rho| \leq 0.7$ and $p \geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is scale-dependent: monotonic at 1.3B but non-monotonic at 340M (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_convergence}). Second, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent spectrum. The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\% attention (${\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).
19	
20	Our contributions are as follows:
21	\begin{itemize}
22	    \item We demonstrate that per-example predictive multiplicity arising from architectural variation---rather than random initialization---spans a task-dependent spectrum ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks spanning three task categories (retrieval, reasoning, mixed) in three recurrent backbones, with magnitude independent of aggregate convergence (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}, \S\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}).
23	    \item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence, and this pattern is scale-dependent (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_convergence}; \S\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}).
24	\end{itemize}
25	
```

Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields three findings. First, attention-driven convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is scale-dependent: monotonic at 1.3B but non-monotonic at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). Second, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent spectrum. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We demonstrate that per-example predictive multiplicity arising from architectural variation---rather than random initialization---spans a task-dependent spectrum ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks spanning three task categories (retrieval, reasoning, mixed) in three recurrent backbones, with magnitude independent of aggregate convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}).\n    \\item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence, and this pattern is scale-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}; \\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nPractitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores, yet architectures that score identically in aggregate may behave differently on individual inputs---with hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction. When attention layers are added to three different recurrent architectures, their LAMBADA performance becomes near-identical: cross-architecture standard deviation drops from 0.024 to 0.005, a 79\\% reduction (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero). Yet this aggregate convergence hides an asymmetry at the per-example level. The three architectures reach similar overall accuracy by helping and hurting different examples with low overlap (pairwise Jaccard at most 0.11, approximately twice the independence baseline). Two models can agree on a benchmark score while disagreeing on which inputs they get right.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \\citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \\citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is scale-dependent: monotonic at 1.3B but non-monotonic at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). Second, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent spectrum. On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled.     "oldString": "    \\item We demonstrate that per-example predictive multiplicity arising from architectural variation---rather than random initialization---spans a task-dependent spectrum ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks spanning three task categories (retrieval, reasoning, mixed) in three recurrent backbones, with magnitude independent of aggregate convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}).",
    "newString": "    \\item Unlike prior studies of seed-driven disagreement within a single architecture \\citep{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, we show that architectural variation---under matched training conditions---produces structured per-example predictive multiplicity with a task-dependent spectrum ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks in three recurrent backbones. This multiplicity is not addressable by seed averaging: the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, and its magnitude is independent of aggregate convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nPractitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores. We find that three recurrent architectures, given identical training and attention configurations, help and hurt almost entirely different examples: pairwise Jaccard overlap is at most 0.10, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence persists even when aggregate scores converge. On LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\% with attention (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by rescuing and disrupting different inputs. Architectures that score identically in aggregate may thus carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \\citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \\citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks---LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$), PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$), and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$)---we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields three findings. First, attention-driven convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is scale-dependent: monotonic at 1.3B but non-monotonic at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). Second, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent spectrum. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We demonstrate that per-example predictive multiplicity arising from architectural variation---rather than random initialization---spans a task-dependent spectrum ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks spanning three task categories (retrieval, reasoning, mixed) in three recurrent backbones, with magnitude independent of aggregate convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}).\n    \\item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence, and this pattern is scale-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}; \\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

_source line 3490 · 2026-05-21T16:01:41.413Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01FuR3rLEtVX9AwujLR81vMa`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores. We find that three recurrent architectures, given identical training and attention configurations, help and hurt almost entirely different examples: pairwise Jaccard overlap is at most 0.10, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence persists even when aggregate scores converge. On LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\% with attention (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the BCa 95\% CI excludes zero), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by rescuing and disrupting different inputs. Architectures that score identically in aggregate may thus carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.
4	
5	This tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.
```
