Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores; architectures that score identically in aggregate may thus carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nPractitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores. We find that three recurrent architectures, given identical training and attention configurations, help and hurt almost entirely different examples: pairwise Jaccard overlap is at most 0.10, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence persists even when aggregate scores converge. On LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\% with attention (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by rescuing and disrupting different inputs. Architectures that score identically in aggregate may thus carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \\citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \\citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks---LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$), PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$), and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$)---we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields three findings. First, attention-driven convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is scale-dependent: monotonic at 1.3B but non-monotonic at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). Second, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent spectrum. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item Unlike prior studies of seed-driven disagreement within a single architecture \\citep{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, we show that architectural variation---under matched training conditions---produces structured per-example predictive multiplicity with a task-dependent spectrum ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks in three recurrent backbones. This multiplicity is not addressable by seed averaging: the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, and its magnitude is independent of aggregate convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}).\n    \\item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence, and this pattern is scale-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}; \\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

1	\documentclass[11pt]{article}
2	
3	\usepackage[review]{acl}
4	
5	\usepackage{times}
6	\usepackage{latexsym}
7	\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}
8	\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}
9	\usepackage{microtype}
10	\usepackage{graphicx}
11	\usepackage{booktabs}
12	\usepackage{amsmath}
13	\usepackage{amssymb}
14	\usepackage{multirow}
15	\usepackage{xcolor}
16	
17	\title{Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Recurrent Architectures}
18	
19	\author{Anonymous}
20	
21	\begin{document}
22	\maketitle
23	
24	\begin{abstract}
25	Hybrid recurrent architectures that interleave attention with linear recurrence are increasingly deployed, yet how attention affects per-example behavior across backbones remains unquantified. Using the controlled model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}---three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales (340M/1.3B)---we evaluate 10 benchmarks and decompose per-example effects on four (LAMBADA, HellaSwag, PIQA, ARC-Easy). Aggregate convergence is task-specific: only LAMBADA shows monotonic variance reduction (79\%), consistent with attention compensating for retrieval differences across recurrent backbones. Per-example predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} varies across tasks ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) while pairwise Jaccard of helped/hurt sets remains uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$). On LAMBADA, aggregate variance decreases gradually while per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level---the two are decoupled. Multiplicity magnitude depends on task type, not attention dose.
26	\end{abstract}
27	
28	\input{sections/introduction}
29	
30	\input{sections/related_work}
31	
32	\input{sections/method}
33	
34	\input{sections/experiments}
35	
36	\input{sections/conclusion}
37	
38	\section*{Limitations}
39	
40	Our analysis has several limitations.
41	
42	\paragraph{Methodological constraints.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable two-tailed $p$-value is approximately 0.008, contributing to the Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ for LAMBADA not reaching conventional significance. Each zoo configuration corresponds to a single training run; we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects, though the $3\times$ Jaccard gap (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}) provides proxy evidence for architecture as the dominant factor. Our attention weight analysis is descriptive, not causal; we compare only two scale points (340M, 1.3B)---a binary observation, not a scaling law.
43	
44	\paragraph{Coverage.} Per-example analysis covers four main benchmarks; BoolQ and WinoGrande are supplementary (Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}, \ref{app:winogrande}). The correlation between $\alpha$ and task accuracy is moderate but not significant ($\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$); HellaSwag (60\%, $\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (73\%, $\alpha = 0.14$) show low $\alpha$ despite varied accuracy, while benchmarks near chance level (BoolQ, WinoGrande) show the highest $\alpha$. We cannot fully exclude accuracy as a confound. We examine three recurrent architectures from one model zoo; other hybrid families may produce different patterns.
45	
46	\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All models are evaluated after pre-training without fine-tuning; per-example effects and the decoupling pattern may not persist after task-specific adaptation.
47	
48	\section*{Ethics Statement}
49	
50	This work analyzes publicly available pretrained language models from the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}. No new data collection, human subjects research, or model training is involved. We identify per-example behavioral differences across architectures, which may inform practitioners about the limitations of aggregate benchmark comparisons but does not introduce new risks.
51	
52	\input{arr_checklist}
53	
54	\bibliography{references}
55	
56	\appendix
57	\input{sections/appendix}
58	
59	\end{document}
60

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We present the first systematic per-example analysis of attention effects in hybrid recurrent models. Aggregate convergence is task-specific (only LAMBADA) and scale-dependent (monotonic at 1.3B, non-monotonic at 340M). Per-example multiplicity spans a task-dependent spectrum ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with Jaccard uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$) regardless of aggregate convergence, suggesting diverse hybrid ensembles may offer complementary coverage. While low Jaccard overlap is suggestive, ensemble gains require empirical validation beyond the scope of this observational study.
4	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01AEK3o8ibnVd6o2miLuJ26r`</summary>

```text
17	
18	Our analysis yields three findings. First, attention-driven convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0,\,-0.7]$; cross-architecture $\sigma$ drops 79\%). LAMBADA \citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\rho| \leq 0.7$ and $p \geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is scale-dependent: monotonic at 1.3B but non-monotonic at 340M (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_convergence}). Second, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent spectrum. The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\% attention (${\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).
19	
20	Our contributions are as follows:
21	\begin{itemize}
22	    \item Unlike prior studies of seed-driven disagreement within a single architecture \citep{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, we show that architectural variation---under matched training conditions---produces structured per-example predictive multiplicity with a task-dependent spectrum ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks in three recurrent backbones. This multiplicity is not addressable by seed averaging: the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, and its magnitude is independent of aggregate convergence (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}, \S\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}).
23	    \item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence, and this pattern is scale-dependent (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_convergence}; \S\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}).
24	\end{itemize}
25	
```

</details>

Our attention weight analysis is descriptive, not causal; we compare only two scale points (340M, 1.3B)---a binary observation, not a scaling law.\n\n\\paragraph{Coverage.} Per-example analysis covers four main benchmarks; BoolQ and WinoGrande are supplementary (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}, \\ref{app:winogrande}). We cannot fully exclude accuracy as a confound. Using the controlled model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}---three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales (340M/1.3B)---we evaluate 10 benchmarks and decompose per-example effects on four (LAMBADA, HellaSwag, PIQA, ARC-Easy). Aggregate convergence is task-specific: only LAMBADA shows monotonic variance reduction (79\\%), consistent with attention compensating for retrieval differences across recurrent backbones. Per-example predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} varies across tasks ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) while pairwise Jaccard of helped/hurt sets remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$). On LAMBADA, aggregate variance decreases gradually while per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level---the two are decoupled. Multiplicity magnitude depends on task type, not attention dose.",
          "+Hybrid recurrent architectures that interleave attention with linear recurrence are increasingly deployed, yet how attention affects per-example behavior across backbones remains unquantified. Using the controlled model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}---three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales (340M/1.3B)---we evaluate 10 benchmarks and decompose per-example effects on four (LAMBADA, HellaSwag, PIQA, ARC-Easy). Per-example predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} varies across tasks ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) while pairwise Jaccard of helped/hurt sets remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$); multiplicity magnitude depends on task type, not attention dose. Aggregate convergence, by contrast, is task-specific: only LAMBADA shows monotonic variance reduction (79\\%), consistent with attention compensating for retrieval differences across recurrent backbones. On LAMBADA, aggregate variance decreases gradually while per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level---the two are decoupled.",
          " \\end{abstract}",
          " ",
          " \\input{sections/introduction}"
        ]

```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
  "old_string": "Our analysis yields three findings. First, attention-driven convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is scale-dependent: monotonic at 1.3B but non-monotonic at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). Second, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent spectrum. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).",
  "new_string": "Our analysis yields three findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent spectrum. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval). Second, attention-driven aggregate convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is scale-dependent: monotonic at 1.3B but non-monotonic at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. The experiments that follow begin with convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}) because it establishes the aggregate backdrop against which per-example divergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}) and decoupling (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) are measured."
}

The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval). Second, attention-driven aggregate convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is scale-dependent: monotonic at 1.3B but non-monotonic at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. The experiments that follow begin with convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}) because it establishes the aggregate backdrop against which per-example divergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}) and decoupling (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) are measured.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nWhen attention layers are interleaved into recurrent architectures, they help and hurt almost entirely different examples---pairwise Jaccard overlap among three hybrid designs is at most 0.10, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by rescuing and disrupting different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores; architectures that score identically in aggregate may thus carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \\citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \\citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks---LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$), PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$), and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$)---we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields three findings. First, attention-driven convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is scale-dependent: monotonic at 1.3B but non-monotonic at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). Second, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent spectrum. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item Unlike prior studies of seed-driven disagreement within a single architecture \\citep{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, we show that architectural variation---under matched training conditions---produces structured per-example predictive multiplicity with a task-dependent spectrum ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks in three recurrent backbones. This multiplicity is not addressable by seed averaging: the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, and its magnitude is independent of aggregate convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}).\n    \\item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence, and this pattern is scale-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}; \\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. 24	We thank the reviewers for their detailed and constructive feedback. We have revised the paper to address each concern. Below, we respond point-by-point, noting specific changes and their locations.
25	
26	\section*{Overview of Changes}
27	
28	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
29	\item New title scoped to observed evidence rather than general claims.
30	\item Abstract and Introduction: Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ now immediately qualifies the 79\% reduction at first mention.
31	\item All instances of ``general property'' replaced with empirically scoped language.

33	\item Cross-benchmark confidence margin analysis added (Appendix): rescue pattern replicates 9/9 on main benchmarks; disruption gradient quantified via coefficient of variation.
34	\item Table~2: $\rho_\sigma$ column renamed to $\rho_\alpha$ (Spearman between attention ratio and ambiguity $\alpha$); LAMBADA value corrected from $-1.0$ to $-0.6$.
35	\item \S4.4 expanded to detail the GDN head-count confound in the main text.
36	\item ARR Responsible NLP Research Checklist added.
37	\item Table~1 footnote explains the $p$-value rounding discrepancy.
38	\item Limitations section strengthened with single-checkpoint caveat.

42	\section*{Detailed Responses}
43	
44	\subsection*{W-CITE (R2-N1): Incorrect citation for model zoo}
45	
46	\reviewer{The citation for the model zoo (wang2025hybrid) has incorrect title and author list.}
47	
48	\response{Fixed. The bib entry now reads ``A Systematic Analysis of Hybrid Linear Attention'' with authors Dustin Wang, Rui-Jie Zhu, Katharopoulos, and Gu.}
49	
50	\location{\texttt{references.bib}, entry \texttt{wang2025hybrid}.}
51	
52	\subsection*{W-CHECKLIST (R2-N2): Missing ARR Responsible NLP Research Checklist}
53	
54	\reviewer{The submission lacks the mandatory ARR Responsible NLP Research Checklist.}
55	
56	\response{Added. The checklist covers all sections (A1--A4, B1--B6, C1--C3, D, E) with specific justifications. Notably, C1 reports the computational budget (single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000, $\sim$5 GPU-hours inference-only) and A3 explicitly notes the scoped claims.}
57	
58	\location{\texttt{arr\_checklist.tex}, included after the Ethics Statement in \texttt{main.tex}.}
59	
60	\subsection*{W-OVERCLAIM (R1-W1, R2-N3): Overclaiming on convergence}
61	
62	\reviewer{The 79\% reduction is presented prominently without immediately noting that the adjusted p-value does not reach significance. The title claims ``Task-Specific Convergence'' which may overstate the strength of evidence.}
63	
64	\response{Three changes address this:}
65	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2em]
66	\item \textbf{Title}: Changed from ``Task-Specific Convergence and Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Recurrent Architectures'' to ``Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Recurrent Architectures.'' The convergence claim is removed from the title.
67	\item \textbf{Abstract}: At first mention of the 79\% reduction, we now write ``though the Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not reach conventional significance.''
68	\item \textbf{Introduction}: The opening hook (79\% reduction) is immediately followed by the same Bonferroni qualification.
69	\end{enumerate}
70	
71	\location{Title (line 1 of \texttt{main.tex}); Abstract (paragraph 1); Introduction \S1 (paragraph 1 and Finding 1).}
72	
73	\subsection*{W-GENERAL (R1-W2): ``General property'' overclaims from limited evidence}
74	
75	\reviewer{Claiming per-example multiplicity is a ``general property of hybrid architectures'' is too strong given only three architectures and two benchmarks.}
76	
77	\response{We have replaced all instances of ``general property'' with empirically scoped language and expanded the per-example analysis from two to four benchmarks (plus two supplementary):}
78	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
79	\item Per-example analysis now covers four benchmarks spanning three task categories: LAMBADA (retrieval), HellaSwag (reasoning), PIQA (reasoning), ARC-Easy (mixed). BoolQ and WinoGrande are reported as supplementary in the Appendix due to structural limitations affecting Jaccard significance.
80	\item Multiplicity is characterized as a task-dependent spectrum: $\alpha$ ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.30 (LAMBADA).
81	\item PIQA and HellaSwag exhibit the lowest multiplicity ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.15$), confirming that multiplicity magnitude is task-dependent rather than noise.
82	\item Within-benchmark $\alpha$ stability verified for all four benchmarks: range 0.004 (HellaSwag), 0.011 (PIQA), 0.025 (ARC-Easy), 0.022 (LAMBADA), all below 0.03 (Appendix, Table~11).
83	\item Conclusion ensemble claim: now qualified with ``if confirmed in additional architectures''
84	\end{itemize}
85	
86	\location{Abstract, \S1, \S3.4, \S4.3 (Table~2), \S4.4, \S5, Limitations, Appendix.}
87	
88	\subsection*{W-GDN (R1-W4): GDN head-count confound}
89	
90	\reviewer{GDN's h3-1 configuration uses 16 attention heads rather than 32, creating a confound. The sensitivity analysis is only in the appendix.}
91	
92	\response{\S4.4 (Robustness) now contains two paragraphs. The first retains the existing sensitivity test: removing GDN weakens $\rho$ from $-1.0$ to $-0.7$ ($p = .233$), while the RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves the decoupling (all hybrid $\sigma \leq 0.008$, Jaccard saturates at 0.086--0.108). The second paragraph, newly added, explains the mechanism: GDN's h3-1 uses 16 attention heads vs.\ 32 in all other configurations, introducing an attention-capacity asymmetry. We state the conclusion explicitly: ``The aggregate convergence result is therefore partially dependent on GDN; the decoupling result is not.''}
93	
94	\location{\S4.4, paragraph 2.}
95	
96	\subsection*{R3-POWER (R1-W3): Low statistical power ($n = 5$)}
97	
98	\reviewer{With $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable p-value is $\sim$0.008, limiting the power of the convergence test.}
99	
100	\response{We acknowledge this as a structural constraint of the model zoo design. It is discussed in three places: (1) the Method section describes the exact permutation test with 120 permutations; (2) \S4.1 reports the BCa bootstrap CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$ as a complementary characterization alongside the $p$-value; (3) the Limitations section (``Statistical power'' paragraph) explicitly states the minimum achievable $p$-value ceiling and its consequences. We agree that this limits the strength of the convergence finding, which is why we have removed convergence from the title and weakened all convergence-related claims (see W-OVERCLAIM above). The per-example multiplicity finding, which is the paper's central contribution, does not depend on the convergence $p$-value.}
101	
102	\location{\S3.5, \S4.1 (paragraph 1), Limitations (``Statistical power'' paragraph).}
103	
104	\subsection*{R3-BINARY (R1-W5): Binary correct/incorrect decomposition}
105	
106	\reviewer{The helped/hurt decomposition uses binary correctness, which may lose information compared to continuous confidence scores.}
107	
108	\response{The binary decomposition is a deliberate methodological choice aligned with the predictive multiplicity framework of Marx et al.\ (2020), which defines multiplicity over discrete prediction outcomes. Additionally, we report continuous-valued analyses that are consistent with the binary results: Cohen's $d$ for pure-model confidence margins ranges from 0.73 to 0.92 between helped and hurt examples on LAMBADA (Appendix, ``Confidence Margin Analysis''), showing that the binary decomposition captures a real separation in the continuous confidence space. We have extended this analysis to all four benchmarks:}
109	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
110	\item \textbf{Rescue pattern (C2)}: Near-miss errors rescued by attention replicate across all three main benchmarks with confidence margin data (9/9 tests significant; $d = 0.13$--$0.17$ on HellaSwag under \texttt{acc\_norm}, $d = 0.51$--$0.60$ on ARC-Easy, $d = 0.73$--$0.77$ on LAMBADA). On BoolQ (binary classification), the rescue signal is weaker but directionally consistent (2/3, $d = 0.16$--$0.36$).
111	\item \textbf{Disruption pattern (C1)}: Effect sizes decrease with answer-space dimensionality: open-ended prediction ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$, LAMBADA) $>$ ARC-Easy ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$) $>$ HellaSwag ($d \approx 0$ under \texttt{acc\_norm}) $\approx$ binary ($d \approx 0$, BoolQ). This gradient broadly tracks the coefficient of variation (CV) of the log-likelihood confidence proxy: LAMBADA (CV $= 0.75$) $>$ ARC-Easy ($0.54$) $>$ HellaSwag ($0.49$) $>$ BoolQ ($0.22$). BoolQ's CV is $3.4\times$ lower than LAMBADA's, leaving insufficient relative spread to distinguish borderline-correct from robustly-correct examples. The asymmetry with C2 is expected: a correct answer in multiple-choice settings can be robust despite low absolute confidence if all alternatives score worse, whereas near-miss errors remain identifiable by relatively high target probability regardless of task format.
112	\end{itemize}
113	Critically, the multiplicity findings themselves---ambiguity $\alpha$ and pairwise Jaccard---do not depend on the confidence margin analysis. Multiplicity is present and statistically significant on all four benchmarks including BoolQ ($\alpha = 0.45$, 12/24 Jaccard pairs significant). The confidence margin provides a partial mechanistic account of \emph{why} certain examples are affected, but the \emph{existence} of per-example multiplicity is established independently.
114	
115	The Jaccard similarity measure applied to the binary sets provides an interpretable metric of cross-architecture overlap that would be less transparent with continuous alternatives.
116	
117	\location{\S4.3 (confidence margin paragraph), Appendix (Confidence Margin Analysis, Cross-Benchmark Extension, BoolQ Supplementary Analysis).}
118	
119	\subsection*{W-ZOO (R1-W6): Reliance on a single model zoo}
120	
121	\reviewer{All results depend on one model zoo; the controlled design limits external validity.}
122	
123	\response{We acknowledge this trade-off explicitly. The controlled design of the zoo---identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters across all configurations---is what allows us to isolate architectural effects from training confounds. This is stated in \S3.1 and in the Limitations section (``Architecture coverage'' paragraph), which now also notes: ``Each configuration in the model zoo corresponds to a single training run; we cannot disentangle architectural effects from seed-specific effects.'' The Conclusion calls for extending the analysis ``beyond these three architectures.'' We believe the controlled design is the appropriate choice for a first systematic study, while recognizing that confirmation across additional model families is needed.}
124	
125	\location{\S3.1, Limitations (``Architecture coverage''), \S5.}
126	
127	\subsection*{Q1: p-value rounding discrepancy (0.100 vs.\ 0.102)}
128	
129	\reviewer{Why does the Bonferroni-adjusted p-value appear as both 0.100 and 0.102 in different places?}
130	
131	\response{We have added a footnote to Table~1 explaining the discrepancy: ``Bonferroni-adjusted $p = 6 \times 2/120 = 0.100$ (exact); 0.102 arises from rounding raw $p$ to .017 before multiplying.'' The value 0.100 is the exact result; 0.102 is a rounding artifact. We now use 0.100 consistently throughout.}
132	
133	\location{Table~1 footnote.}
134	
135	\subsection*{Q5: Single checkpoint per configuration}
136	
137	\reviewer{Each model zoo configuration is a single training run. Can you disentangle architecture from seed?}
138	
139	\response{We cannot, and we now state this explicitly in the Limitations section: ``Each configuration in the model zoo corresponds to a single training run; we cannot disentangle architectural effects from seed-specific effects.'' This is a limitation of the zoo design. Disentangling the two would require multiple seeds per architecture-attention configuration, which is beyond the scope of the available zoo. We note, however, that the zoo's controlled design (shared data, hyperparameters) reduces other confounds, making architecture the most salient axis of variation.}
140	
141	\location{Limitations (``Architecture coverage'' paragraph).}
142	
143	\subsection*{W-REFS (R2-W2): Additional related work citations}
144

BoolQ and WinoGrande are reported as supplementary in the Appendix due to structural limitations affecting Jaccard significance.\n\\item Multiplicity is characterized as a task-dependent spectrum: $\\alpha$ ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.30 (LAMBADA).\n\\item PIQA and HellaSwag exhibit the lowest multiplicity ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.15$), confirming that multiplicity magnitude is task-dependent rather than noise.\n\\item Within-benchmark $\\alpha$ stability verified for all four benchmarks: range 0.004 (HellaSwag), 0.011 (PIQA), 0.025 (ARC-Easy), 0.022 (LAMBADA), all below 0.03 (Appendix, Table~11).\n\\item Conclusion ensemble claim: now qualified with ``if confirmed in additional architectures''\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\location{Abstract, \\S1, \\S3.4, \\S4.3 (Table~2), \\S4.4, \\S5, Limitations, Appendix.}\n\n\\subsection*{W-GDN (R1-W4): GDN head-count confound}\n\n\\reviewer{GDN's h3-1 configuration uses 16 attention heads rather than 32, creating a confound. The sensitivity analysis is only in the appendix.}\n\n\\response{\\S4.4 (Robustness) now contains two paragraphs. The first retains the existing sensitivity test: removing GDN weakens $\\rho$ from $-1.0$ to $-0.7$ ($p = .233$), while the RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves the decoupling (all hybrid $\\sigma \\leq 0.008$, Jaccard saturates at 0.086--0.108). The second paragraph, newly added, explains the mechanism: GDN's h3-1 uses 16 attention heads vs.\\ 32 in all other configurations, introducing an attention-capacity asymmetry. We state the conclusion explicitly: ``The aggregate convergence result is therefore partially dependent on GDN; the decoupling result is not.''}\n\n\\location{\\S4.4, paragraph 2.}\n\n\\subsection*{R3-POWER (R1-W3): Low statistical power ($n = 5$)}\n\n\\reviewer{With $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable p-value is $\\sim$0.008, limiting the power of the convergence test.}\n\n\\response{We acknowledge this as a structural constraint of the model zoo design. It is discussed in three places: (1) the Method section describes the exact permutation test with 120 permutations; (2) \\S4.1 reports the BCa bootstrap CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$ as a complementary characterization alongside the $p$-value; (3) the Limitations section (``Statistical power'' paragraph) explicitly states the minimum achievable $p$-value ceiling and its consequences. We agree that this limits the strength of the convergence finding, which is why we have removed convergence from the title and weakened all convergence-related claims (see W-OVERCLAIM above). The per-example multiplicity finding, which is the paper's central contribution, does not depend on the convergence $p$-value.}\n\n\\location{\\S3.5, \\S4.1 (paragraph 1), Limitations (``Statistical power'' paragraph).}\n\n\\subsection*{R3-BINARY (R1-W5): Binary correct/incorrect decomposition}\n\n\\reviewer{The helped/hurt decomposition uses binary correctness, which may lose information compared to continuous confidence scores.}\n\n\\response{The binary decomposition is a deliberate methodological choice aligned with the predictive multiplicity framework of Marx et al.\\ (2020), which defines multiplicity over discrete prediction outcomes. Additionally, we report continuous-valued analyses that are consistent with the binary results: Cohen's $d$ for pure-model confidence margins ranges from 0.73 to 0.92 between helped and hurt examples on LAMBADA (Appendix, ``Confidence Margin Analysis''), showing that the binary decomposition captures a real separation in the continuous confidence space. We have extended this analysis to all four benchmarks:}\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]\n\\item \\textbf{Rescue pattern (C2)}: Near-miss errors rescued by attention replicate across all three main benchmarks with confidence margin data (9/9 tests significant; $d = 0.13$--$0.17$ on HellaSwag under \\texttt{acc\\_norm}, $d = 0.51$--$0.60$ on ARC-Easy, $d = 0.73$--$0.77$ on LAMBADA). On BoolQ (binary classification), the rescue signal is weaker but directionally consistent (2/3, $d = 0.16$--$0.36$).\n\\item \\textbf{Disruption pattern (C1)}: Effect sizes decrease with answer-space dimensionality: open-ended prediction ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$, LAMBADA) $>$ ARC-Easy ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$) $>$ HellaSwag ($d \\approx 0$ under \\texttt{acc\\_norm}) $\\approx$ binary ($d \\approx 0$, BoolQ). This gradient broadly tracks the coefficient of variation (CV) of the log-likelihood confidence proxy: LAMBADA (CV $= 0.75$) $>$ ARC-Easy ($0.54$) $>$ HellaSwag ($0.49$) $>$ BoolQ ($0.22$). BoolQ's CV is $3.4\\times$ lower than LAMBADA's, leaving insufficient relative spread to distinguish borderline-correct from robustly-correct examples. The asymmetry with C2 is expected: a correct answer in multiple-choice settings can be robust despite low absolute confidence if all alternatives score worse, whereas near-miss errors remain identifiable by relatively high target probability regardless of task format.\n\\end{itemize}\nCritically, the multiplicity findings themselves---ambiguity $\\alpha$ and pairwise Jaccard---do not depend on the confidence margin analysis. Multiplicity is present and statistically significant on all four benchmarks including BoolQ ($\\alpha = 0.45$, 12/24 Jaccard pairs significant). The confidence margin provides a partial mechanistic account of \\emph{why} certain examples are affected, but the \\emph{existence} of per-example multiplicity is established independently.\n\nThe Jaccard similarity measure applied to the binary sets provides an interpretable metric of cross-architecture overlap that would be less transparent with continuous alternatives.\n\n\\location{\\S4.3 (confidence margin paragraph), Appendix (Confidence Margin Analysis, Cross-Benchmark Extension, BoolQ Supplementary Analysis).}\n\n\\subsection*{W-ZOO (R1-W6): Reliance on a single model zoo}\n\n\\reviewer{All results depend on one model zoo; the controlled design limits external validity.}\n\n\\response{We acknowledge this trade-off explicitly. The controlled design of the zoo---identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters across all configurations---is what allows us to isolate architectural effects from training confounds. This is stated in \\S3.1 and in the Limitations section (``Architecture coverage'' paragraph), which now also notes: ``Each configuration in the model zoo corresponds to a single training run; we cannot disentangle architectural effects from seed-specific effects.'' The Conclusion calls for extending the analysis ``beyond these three architectures.'' We believe the controlled design is the appropriate choice for a first systematic study, while recognizing that confirmation across additional model families is needed.}\n\n\\location{\\S3.1, Limitations (``Architecture coverage''), \\S5.}\n\n\\subsection*{Q1: p-value rounding discrepancy (0.100 vs.\\ 0.102)}\n\n\\reviewer{Why does the Bonferroni-adjusted p-value appear as both 0.100 and 0.102 in different places?}\n\n\\response{We have added a footnote to Table~1 explaining the discrepancy: ``Bonferroni-adjusted $p = 6 \\times 2/120 = 0.100$ (exact); 0.102 arises from rounding raw $p$ to .017 before multiplying.'' The value 0.100 is the exact result; 0.102 is a rounding artifact. We now use 0.100 consistently throughout.}\n\n\\location{Table~1 footnote.}\n\n\\subsection*{Q5: Single checkpoint per configuration}\n\n\\reviewer{Each model zoo configuration is a single training run. Can you disentangle architecture from seed?}\n\n\\response{We cannot, and we now state this explicitly in the Limitations section: ``Each configuration in the model zoo corresponds to a single training run; we cannot disentangle architectural effects from seed-specific effects.'' This is a limitation of the zoo design. Disentangling the two would require multiple seeds per architecture-attention configuration, which is beyond the scope of the available zoo. We note, however, that the zoo's controlled design (shared data, hyperparameters) reduces other confounds, making architecture the most salient axis of variation.}\n\n\\location{Limitations (``Architecture coverage'' paragraph).}\n\n\\subsection*{W-REFS (R2-W2): Additional related work citations}\n\n\\reviewer{Several relevant references are missing from the related work discussion.}\n\n\\response{We have substantially expanded the citation coverage. In addition to Sellam et al.\\ (ICLR 2022; MultiBERTs) and Fehlauer et al.\\ (EMNLP 2025) added in Round~2, we now cite five additional references:}\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]\n\\item \\textbf{Predictive Multiplicity paragraph}: Du et al.\\ (2025; reconciliation under multiplicity), Haghighat et al.\\ (2026; Rashomon-set resolution), and Gorbett \\& Jana (2026; cross-model disagreement as label-free correctness signal).\n\\item \\textbf{Hybrid Architectures paragraph}: Ali et al.\\ (2025; implicit attention in Mamba) and Lahoti et al.\\ (2026; Mamba-3).\n\\item \\textbf{Appendix~D}: Ali et al.\\ (2025) cited as methodological background for attention weight analysis.\n\\item \\textbf{\\S4.3}: Gorbett \\& Jana (2026) cited for the practical relevance of per-example disagreement.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\location{\\S2 (``Predictive Multiplicity'' and ``Hybrid Recurrent Architectures'' paragraphs), \\S4.3, Appendix~D, \\texttt{references.bib}.}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

          "-\\item \\textbf{Abstract}: At first mention of the 79\\% reduction, we now write ``though the Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not reach conventional significance.''",
          "-\\item \\textbf{Introduction}: The opening hook (79\\% reduction) is immediately followed by the same Bonferroni qualification.",
          "+\\item \\textbf{Abstract}: Rewritten to lead with per-example multiplicity ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$, Jaccard $\\leq 0.10$); convergence introduced with ``by contrast'' as secondary finding.",
          "+\\item \\textbf{Introduction}: Opening hook rewritten from convergence to per-example multiplicity (Jaccard $\\leq 0.10$ is the first number readers encounter); convergence demoted to background in sentence~2. Findings reordered: First = multiplicity spectrum, Second = convergence.",
          "+\\item \\textbf{Conclusion}: Likewise rewritten to multiplicity-first; convergence follows with ``by contrast.''",
          " \\end{enumerate}",

  "old_string": "\\response{We acknowledge this as a structural constraint of the model zoo design. It is discussed in three places: (1) the Method section describes the exact permutation test with 120 permutations; (2) \\S4.1 reports the BCa bootstrap CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$ as a complementary characterization alongside the $p$-value; (3) the Limitations section (``Statistical power'' paragraph) explicitly states the minimum achievable $p$-value ceiling and its consequences. We agree that this limits the strength of the convergence finding, which is why we have removed convergence from the title and weakened all convergence-related claims (see W-OVERCLAIM above). The per-example multiplicity finding, which is the paper's central contribution, does not depend on the convergence $p$-value.}\n\n\\location{\\S3.5, \\S4.1 (paragraph 1), Limitations (``Statistical power'' paragraph).}",
  "new_string": "\\response{We acknowledge this as a structural constraint of the model zoo design. It is discussed in three places: (1) the Method section describes the exact permutation test with 120 permutations; (2) \\S4.1 reports the BCa bootstrap CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$ as a complementary characterization alongside the $p$-value; (3) the Limitations section (``Statistical power'' paragraph) explicitly states the minimum achievable $p$-value ceiling and its consequences. We agree that this limits the strength of the convergence finding, which is why we have removed convergence from the title and weakened all convergence-related claims (see W-OVERCLAIM above). The per-example multiplicity finding, which is the paper's central contribution, does not depend on the convergence $p$-value. Additionally, bootstrap 95\\% CIs on $\\alpha$ confirm the multiplicity spectrum ordering (all adjacent-benchmark CIs non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag), and subsample sensitivity analysis shows $\\alpha$ stabilizes at $n \\geq 500$ (CI width $< 0.08$; Appendix~\\S G).}\n\n\\location{\\S3.5, \\S4.1 (paragraph 1), Limitations (``Statistical power'' paragraph), Appendix~\\S G.}"
}
